ParlaMint-RS_2009-06-09-0.u21678	We move to the 7th extraordinary meeting of the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia in 2009. Dear ladies and gentlemen of the People's Parliament, we continue our work by voting on the single agenda of the 7th extraordinary session of the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia in 2009. Before we move to decision-making, we need to determine the quorum. According to Article 86(5), The Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, a quorum for the work of the National Assembly in the voting day, exist if at least 126 MPs are present at the meeting of the National Assembly. The application of the electronic voting system has shown that 129 MPs are present in the hall, i.e. that more than 126 MPs are present and that therefore there are conditions for deciding. We move on to the 1st item of the agenda: – Proposal for a DECISION ON THE PARTICIPATION OF PROFESSIONAL MEMBER STATES IN THE NORWAY CONTENTION IN THE SINGLE BUSINESS OF THE UNITED Nations IN THE REPUBLIC OF CHAD AND CENTRAL AFRISH REPUBLIC (MINURCAT) Since the National Assembly has carried out a single search on the proposal for a decision, we move to a decision. I am voting on the Proposal for a decision on the participation of professional members of the Sanitary Ministry of Defence and the Serbian Army as part of the Norwegian contingent in the United Nations Peace Mission in the Republic of Chad and Central African Republic (MINURCAT) for 129 out of 129 MPs. I find that the National Assembly adopted a proposal for a decision on the participation of professional members of the Sanitary Service of the Ministry of Defence and the Serbian Army as part of the Norwegian Contingent in the United Nations Peace Mission in the Republic of Chad and the Central African Republic. We move on to the decision of the National Assembly on the injuries of the Rules of Procedure to which MPs pointed during the session. Since the session shows violations of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, I remind you of the provision of Articles 100, 6 and 10. The Rules of Procedure, according to which, even after the explanation of the President of the National Assembly, the MP considers that the infringement has been committed, the National Assembly will, without delay, declare itself in the day of the vote. In agreement with the provisions of the Rules of Procedure, we move to a decision. National MP Milos Aligrudic, at a session on 3 June 2009, at 1800 hours, pointed to a violation of article 101. Rules of Procedure. Please state whether or not the said Rules of Procedure have been injured. For one, against one, a abstentioned one, did not vote 126 out of 129 MPs. I find that the National Assembly has decided that it is not a injured member of the Rules of Procedure. Since the National Assembly has conducted an examination of the single agenda item of this session and its decision on it, pursuant to Article 99. Rules of Procedure, I hereby conclude the session of the 7th extraordinary session of the National Assembly of the Republic of Serbia in 2009. Please, at 11.00 a.m., we'll be in this same numerous conditions here. (The session is over at 10:30. )

All permits and approvals for construction are required for a plot outside the sanitary zone of the water catchment area of drinking water, and the construction is started on a completely different plot, within the sanitary zone, right next to the water station, in a legally prohibited place. An alarming warning from the citizens of India, led by the ecological movement, that an illegally built factory would endanger a regional water-related area of drinking water, fraud was discovered, but the factory was already placed under the roof. The water industry inspection has brought a solution that prohibits any construction and works to connect the collectors of technical waste water with the main collector of municipal sewers. The reason is more than justifiable, because if the technological wastewaters of Bulgaria's factories are combined with municipal sewage, 400,000 kilos of lead will be poured out in the Danube annually from Batajnica. It says in the environmental assessment study, submitted by Bulgarian investors to the Regional Secretariat for the Protection of the Environment and Sustainable Development for adoption. According to the Construction Act, this study is a prerequisite for the start of any industrial building. In the case of which we write, the factory has already been built, and the study has not yet been adopted. How is that possible? It is possible; when local self-government is corrupt, construction can start and end without any valid document or consent. It is not enough for only one state authority to be at the level of the task; the case described indicates it in the best possible way. When the fraud in the construction of a Bulgarian factory was discovered, it was logical that the municipal public prosecutor acted on official duty, to file criminal charges against all actors in fraud, from municipal, to regional level, which actively or passively enabled the construction of this factory. The criminal proceedings were not even initiated by the Republic Public Prosecutor for the control of organised crime, although the case was described by the school's example of the preparation of actors from the sphere of crime, politics, executive authorities. ParlaMint-RS_2009-06-09-0.u21704	President-in-Office, ladies and gentlemen of the People's Parliament, today is an extraordinary honor for me to have the opportunity to present to you the proposal for a law on regional development. This, in my ministry's view, is the most important law in the field of economic development proposed to the Assembly for adoption this year. Why is this an important law? Because it is one of the important steps towards Serbia's decentralisation, because it helps to establish institutions and capacities that will allow the definition of development priorities to be defined in places where people live, therefore, in regions, areas in the local region, and because the implementation of important projects will depend most on those most concerned, which are citizens themselves living in individual cities. Serbia is one of the few countries that has not defined the law on regional development. We've been working on this proposal for a long time. For two years, the ministry has worked; since we formed the Ministry of Economy and Regional Development, the work on this law has begun. We have included a number of expert consultants in this work, from experts from the European Commission, the European Agency for Reconstruction, through domestic experts. Of course, we have also consulted with representatives of different municipalities who have had some experience in the current period in the process of their development and have installed all of this in the draft that is in front of you. The legal basis for the adoption of this law is the Constitution of Serbia, two of its members. These are members of 94th and 97th, as the Constitution of Serbia states that the Republic of Serbia is looking after balanced and sustainable regional development, in accordance with the law. The law that defines and enables balanced and sustainable regional development is the one that lies before you today. In Article 97. The Serbian Constitution, attitude 12, is also defined as the development of the Republic of Serbia, policies and measures to promote the smooth development of certain parts of the Republic of Serbia, including the development of underdeveloped areas, a commitment that the government should legally regulate, and we do so by this law. We also have an obligation under the EU Association Agreement, in Article 113. Agreements to regulate the area of regional development in line with European standards. We've been given that agreement four years to regulate that area. Given that we signed the agreement a year ago, it is our interest to arrange this area as soon as possible, and that is why today this law is before you. I said at the beginning that this is an important step in decentralisation of Serbia. The first step legally arranged in that direction, on a proposal from the Permanent Conference of Cities and municipalities, which the government then adopted in 2006, was the Law on the financing of local self-government. This is a second important step, so the adoption of the Regional Development Act. A third important step will be the adoption of a law that will regulate the return of property to local self-government. The three laws in the package provide a financial basis for Serbia's real decentralisation. In the future, Serbia's political decentralisation will also be said, but the law does not deal with it, or its range is in economic decentralisation and in enabling Serbia to develop more evenly. So, one of the reasons why this law is passed is to allow for the more balanced development of all areas of Serbia, given that Serbia today is a country that has a very pronounced regional disparity, that the relationship between the most developed and the most underdeveloped unit of local self-government in Serbia, even if one by 15, is the difference in regional development, is measured by the index of developmental threat, one to seven, and unfortunately, the tendency for richer municipalities to become even richer, and those who are poor leave them and the few professional staff and go to the richer ones, so they are even more poor, so that it is a tendency which, certainly, must be resolved through some kind of state intervention. State intervention consists of helping those who are weaker and professionally, and organisationally, and financially, to improve their development and to stop this trend of dispersion, i.e. to reduce disparities. Of course, this is a feature of all the countries in the world. All countries in the world have a problem of uneven development and all countries that want a serious economy have thus regulated institutions that allow these differences to be reduced. One of the consequences of unbalanced regional development is the departure of the population from areas that are poor. These are population migrations. I'll give you data on the population movement in certain municipalities in the last 36 years, in the period since 1971, a census from 1971, until 2007, when we had the latest data. For example, in the municipality of Crna Trava, the number of inhabitants was reduced in this period by as much as 80%, in the Shop, 55%, in Gadjin Han –54%, Babusnica – 53%, Medvedja lost 50% of the population, Bosilegrad – 50%, Žabari – 47.3%, Rekovac – 46.6%, Mali Crnić – 43.6%, Razanj – 42% of the population, Kučevo – 40%, Svrljig – 40%, Bolovac – 39%, Bela Palanka – 38.6%, Nova Crnja – 38%, Žitište – 37%, Kursumlija – 37%, Negotin – 36%, Bojnik – 35.5%, from Knjaževec, 35% of the population went to these 30 and some years, Knić – 35%, Petrovac – 35% and Golubac – 35%. So, from all these municipalities, the population emigrated; some went abroad, while others went to larger cities. At the same time, the number of inhabitants in those cities that were more developed has increased. Most of all in Novi Sad, 54% of Novi Sad had a population inflow over the past 36 years, then Novi Pazar – 46% of the population. There are also cities, municipalities that are not large, but which, by their development, have obviously attracted a part of the population. Stara Pazova, even by 65% increase in population, then from larger towns, Kragujevac, 34% increase in population, Belgrade – 33%, Nis – 32%. Of some smaller municipalities, Sremski Karlovci – 25%, Indji – 21%, Vrnjačka Banja – 21%. Vranje, regardless of what is not fully developed, belongs to so-called. The third group of municipalities developed, had a 21% population growth, as everyone around them was still in a worse position and emigrated towards Vranje. Smederevo – 20% population growth, Užice – 19.5%, Caves – 19.3%, Beocina – 18.8%, Čačak – 18.7%, Pančevo – 13.5%, Kraljevo – 13%, Shabac – 12% and Kruševac – 9%. It exhausts a list of cities that have increased population in the last 36 years. In addition to leaving the population from less developed municipalities and regions, due to insufficient levels of development, this is all manifested in a large pay gap, in the average salaries in these municipalities. The profit range, currently among the most developed municipalities, according to the latest data this year is Novi Belgrade, where the average salary was between January and April this year 48,700 dinars net, compared to the municipality that has the least average salary, Bela Palanko, with 12,451 dinar net salary, is even 4:1. These are the reasons why the population from these poor municipalities and regions is leaving. But in time, it is also a national problem, because if the empty territory of eastern Serbia, southern Serbia and other territories where there is a smaller degree of development, we have a problem in how to preserve the country's national sovereignty at all if we do not have a population. This is why it is the last time through this law to allow far more fair distribution and investments related to economic development, i.e. to help those regions that are at risk, as well as municipalities within the region. It is a policy, a regional policy of the European Union. The example, from the EU structural funds, in this current budget, relating to the period from 2007 to 2013, will be allocated 231 billion euros for undeveloped regions and only 49 billion euros for developed ones. So the ratio is almost 5-1. More money is allocated to undeveloped regions. In the EU, these are mainly regions in Central and Eastern Europe, Spain, Greece, although there are some parts of the UK. As for other countries that I would say, in this decade, have passed such and similar laws, I would mention that Finland passed similar legislation in 2003, the Czech Republic in 2000, Latvia – 2002, Slovenia – 1997, but changed it in 2005, when it adopted the latest version, Bulgaria – 2004, Slovakia – 2000, and then changed it to amendments in 2008, Poland – 1999, Hungary – 1996. A year. So almost all the countries that have joined the EU have passed these laws, because today the EU is also a country of the region, given that there are no borders between the national states of the European Union, economic co-operation is increasingly taking place not among the national states, but directly between the regions. In this way, regions have the opportunity to meet the specific needs of the population living in them, which are different from the region to the region and do not have to have anything to do with what the needs of the national state are in general. That is how this cooperation is intensified. As far as the structure of this law is concerned, we have given basic definitions to which we regulate the law, and one of the basic definitions is the definition of the region, given that it is the law on regional development. The region is defined as a statistical functional territorial continent, consisting of one or more areas, and is established for the purposes of planning and implementing regional development policy, in accordance with the nomenclature of statistical territorial units. It is, in particular, the nomenclature of statistical territorial units according to the classification of the European Statistical Office, etc. Eurostat, according to which there are three levels to define the region. The first level is Nalc 1, these are areas where at least three million people live, and for the purposes of this law, the whole of Serbia is this basic region, the level of Nalc 1. Also, very important, I would say, the most important continent for regional development planning is the Nalc 2-level regions of European statistics; they have a minimum population of 800,000, and a maximum of three million people, according to the European classification, while there is an even lower level, that is defined as an area in our law, which is the territorial continents that have between 150,000 and 800,000 inhabitants. It is at these levels that, on the one hand, development planning is being carried out, and, on the other hand, implementation is being carried out, i.e. the implementation of regional development policy. Starting with this definition, for the purpose of encouraging regional development, we have laid down by law, namely to determine the following regions in Serbia, seven of them: the Region of Vojvodina, the Belgrade region, the Western region, the Eastern region, the Central region, the South region and the Kosovo Region and the Metohija. I note once again that the regions defined by the law are statistical regions, have no legal subjectivity, or are administrative regions. This law defines only statistical regions. The very statistical regionalisation, based on this law, will be adopted by the Government, on a proposal from the Republic Statistics Institute, and after obtaining an opinion, i.e. the consent of European statistics, or Eurostat. We did not go into these details here, given that we need the consent of European statistics. That's why we left it to be regulated by a sublegal act, but we have defined these seven regions as the basis for encouraging regional development. The authorities are, as I said, units within the region. Each region consists of several areas, and each area essentially includes more current districts. So we didn't want to go into the law at all to regulate Serbia's territorial organisation. The law is a law for itself, and it deals with other issues. We've come from that law and installed that premise into the basic postulates of this law. The law also lays down criteria for grading municipalities and regions according to the level of development. According to the European methodology, undeveloped regions are considered those whose gross domestic product per capita is below 75% of the national gross domestic product. So, below 75% of the average. Those who have more than 75% compared to the average national gross domestic product are considered relatively developed regions. It also introduces a classification of municipalities, i.e. local self-government units, according to the degree of development. Four groups of municipalities are defined by this law – those who have a degree of development above the republican average, those whose level of development is from 80% to 100% of the republican average, the third group is those whose level of development is between 60% and 80% of the republican average, while the fourth group of municipalities are those whose level of development is below 60% of the republican average. At the moment, there are 40 municipalities in Serbia, unfortunately, which fall within this most underdeveloped, fourth group of municipalities, below 60% of the republican average. The problem is that no system conditions have been put in place so far for greater and more organised assistance to these municipalities. This law regulates not only institutions to plan and finance regional development and help less developed municipalities, but also how to allocate funds in the coming years. In what way? Every year, you MPs are making a budget for next year. As part of that budget, we have, for example, allocated some funds for less developed areas. In particular, about 4.5 billion dinars were allocated to the most underdeveloped municipalities this year, with 2.5 billion dinars of cheap loans to encourage entrepreneurship, we are talking about loans granted through the Development Fund, for a period of seven years, with interest of 0.5% year-on-year and three years of starting periods, and two billion dinars have been allocated under the NIP for the 40 most underdeveloped municipalities in Serbia. As of next year, if this law is passed, the system will be different. The government will determine the total amount of funds for regional development. Then, under this law, by 31 October each year, the level of development of each individual municipality, each of these seven regions, will be established according to the criteria given in the law. On this basis, this mass of funds will be allocated exclusively according to objective criteria, i.e. according to the degree of development. This means that the largest of these municipalities from the fourth group, four times more than those from another group of municipalities, while the most developed municipalities, essentially, unless they are implemented by a national project, will be based on themselves, because they can develop themselves. So, the government will help in the future only the second, third and fourth groups of municipalities, with the second group of municipalities, those whose level of development is from 80% to 100% of the republican average, being obliged to co-finance appropriate projects, and interest rates for loans to encourage employment and entrepreneurship will be slightly more than interest rates for less developed areas. In any case, when the mass of funds to promote regional development is determined, the distribution will depend exclusively on this region's development classification and the least developed areas, such as the EU example, will receive 4-5 times more funding than those which may also not be in excellent condition, but are still 2-3 spears above these most underdeveloped. As for the status of underdeveloped areas, those units of local self-government that have a demographic decline of more than 50% are included in those areas, and this law provides that local self-government units from KIM are also classified in highly underdeveloped areas and that they have this special incentive regime. As for the organisation of institutions and the functioning of incentives regulated by this law, I have already said there are three levels: national, regional and regional. At national level, the National Council for Regional Development is formed, as well as the National Agency for Regional Development. The Council creates a policy of regional development, the Agency implements a policy of regional development. Seven regional development agencies are also formed under the same structure, as well as seven regional councils. Councils at regional level also create a policy of regional development, and agencies implement. To explain what that actually means, I'm going to take advantage of the example. When this law is passed, the priorities for NIP projects will no longer be established by the Republic government, will be established by defining their priorities, I apologize, regional agencies and regional councils to define their priorities. If something is to be built in eastern Serbia, some traffic station, not to be asked by the Republic government, which is a traffic station, it will be decided by regional councils and regional agencies. Otherwise, the law stipulates that most of the regional agencies' boards and most of the members of the regional council are representatives of the local self-government of the region, thus ensuring decentralisation in planning, in defining priorities, and later in monitoring, or in implementing. So the advice will propose, define priorities, while the agencies will implement them. The Agency has, whether it is national, republican, or districtal, two directions of action provided for by this law. One course of action of the agency will be different infrastructure projects of regional, district or local character. The second level of action is to encourage employment and entrepreneurship, therefore, to encourage the development of small and medium-sized enterprises. We do not foresee this law establishing those institutions that already exist. Some of the institutions I've already listed already exist. There's a district association. They have different names in different areas. Today there are 16 district associations. I'll let you know in which cities they all work. Some function more successfully, some less successful. According to the European Commission and its relevant institutions, the most successful regional development agency is currently the Regional Forest Development Agency, from Pomorava, based in Kragujevac. However, the regional agency for the development of Banat, Zrenjanin, the Regional Centre for the Development of the Zlatibor County, Užica, and the Regional Agency for the Development of the Raško and Moraviga County, in the Kingdom, also have very good estimates. At the same time, the four agencies have only met the conditions so far that they have both fields of action – to deal with and develop entrepreneurship and to be trained to propose and monitor the realisation of infrastructure projects. The remaining 12 district associations that exist today will have to, within six months of the entry into force of this law, transform and meet the criteria that this law and sublegal acts will define. It is the Regional Development Agency in Subotica, Novi Sad and Sombor, which is specific to its establishment by the government of time. Under the law, the government will no longer be able to own any district association, but will have to be local self-government. In other words, the district associations must be owned by those who are at the bar – local self-government, local chambers, regional chambers, local businesses. Therefore, this law also allows for a public private partnership when forming district associations. Also, we have regional agencies in Belgrade, Novi Pazar, Leskovac, Vranja, Sapac, Zajecar, Kruševec, Nis and Pozarevac. They have different names, but essentially, these are these district associations that are defined by law. The law provides for regional agencies to credentialise district agencies, i.e. associations. These seven regional agencies will perform, for a period of five years, the accreditation of district associations. How's she gonna do her credentials? Those district associations, which provide results, which benefit citizens, which also have the support of European pre-accession funds, precisely because they are efficient and carry out planned projects, will be accredited. Those who don't meet in work, in practice, these standards, will lose accreditation. This system is designed so that district associations, which can also be formed by public-private partnerships, are constantly motivated to compete with each other and achieve the best results, i.e. to carry out as many projects and employ as many people in concrete areas as possible. In the first two years alone, the accreditation of district associations will be carried out by the National Agency, and after the first two years, it is left to regional agencies and those in the coming period of the regional associations. The highest level for creating a regional development policy under this law will be the National Council for Regional Development. He'll have a president and 19 members. In addition to the minister in charge of regional development, there will be ministers in charge of finance, environment and spatial planning, infrastructure, state administration and local self-government, the National Investment Plan, the sustainable development of underdeveloped areas, labour and social policy, as well as the KIM Minister, who will also be the representative of the KIM Region of the National Council. Also, members of this national council will be president of the Executive Council of the AP Vojvodina, as representative of the Vojvodina region, mayor of Belgrade, which will represent the Belgrade region, representatives of the regional councils of the Western, Eastern, Central and South regions, and then, the president of the Permanent Conference of Cities and Municipalities, the national co-ordinator for co-ordinating the use of EU pre-accession funds. This council, by invitation, can also be attended by experts, experts in this area, without the right to vote. That would lead to a slow end to the interpretation of the law. The law should help to form the necessary institutions, which will be able to withdraw adequate funding for regional development, as well as for helping regions, areas and municipalities that are less developed. The financing of regional development will take place from the budget of the Republic of Serbia, the AP budget, the city of Belgrade, the unit of local self-government. Of course, a very important source of funding will be pre-accession EU funds, because, in order to be able to use these funds more intensively, one of the preconditions is the adoption of such a law, then donations and other sources, in accordance with the law, including loans. As for the National Agency for Regional Development, it will be formed by an existing agency for small and medium-sized enterprises and from the existing sector of the Ministry of Economy and Regional Development for the implementation of infrastructure projects. Currently, there is a Regional Development Sector in the Ministry of Economy, one of which deals with regional development policy, and another with implementation of infrastructure projects. It's not the place to implement infrastructure projects in the government, it's the agencies. As a result, the government's jobs will be abolished and will move to the National Agency for Regional Development, as will the Republic Agency for Small and Medium Enterprises Development and will essentially be transferred to this national agency. The agency will be formed, basically, by these two institutions, the Small and Medium Enterprises Development Agency and by the Sector for the Implementation of Infrastructure Projects at the Ministry of Economy and Regional Development. The new agencies, which will be formed by this law, are these regional agencies, seven regional agencies, however, will be key to implementing regional development policies. Without them, the law wouldn't make sense. That's why we felt it was necessary, as in other countries, that there were regional agencies. These agencies will form the government, however, is the idea that they too, over time, become agencies that will have a joint public-private partnership, with respect to the current regional development agencies, i.e. areas and associations, as we have called them and defined them by this law. As it does, in Sombor, the state capital agency of the Republic government is formed and cannot remain, but will have to take over most local self-government, or partners from the locals, so in time, and regional agencies will have to have, as they do now, most members of the boards of directors from the unity of local self-government, and property will also have to meet that structure in the future. The ownership structure is not so important for the implementation of this law, it is important that the money is distributed according to the criteria defined by this law and according to the degree of development. In the Ministry of Economy and Regional Development, we believe that the resources allocated to the development of less developed areas are not enough. If we count the municipalities of the third and fourth groups and add up all the budgets that are available this year, for example, it is below 100m euros. This is not enough and this cannot speed up regional development, i.e. it cannot accelerate the development of less developed areas. It must be a medium-term goal to allocate at least 2% of the Republic's annual budget to regional development, and then, within that mass of separate funds, four to five times more to the most underdeveloped municipalities and less developed regions. Only when we have such budget allocations, including additional funds from EU pre-accession funds, can we be sure that with words written in the Constitution, on the obligation of equal regional development, we will move to action, and that, even this law cannot confirm, is only a practice of law enforcement that can be confirmed. I must say, we've worked with great enthusiasm on this law. We know that he will help those who have been forgotten so far, neglected, to rise. We know there's going to be a lot of problems with his conduct. The biggest problem will not be financial resources, the biggest problem will be knowledge, which, unfortunately, is not the very ones who are least developed. That is why we have made such a structure, that in the beginning, until the least developed municipalities are raised on their feet, not only 100% of our funding for project implementation, but also project development, and that, in agreement with the representatives of the least developed local self-government and regions, we help to jointly set the right priorities. I expect that we will have great support in implementing the law from the European Union itself, because it is also in the interest of Serbia to create an economic structure that is approximate to the association it wants to enter, which is the EU. Thank you for your attention, and I hope we have the support to pass this law.

Today we have a law that defines the area of work of the association in Serbia, which is completely anachron, was passed in 1982, still talks about working people, etc., about self-management, and it was only then to wait until the adoption of the law on associations, which is held at the session of this session, to discuss this law. As far as the importance of the regional development law is concerned, unfortunately, I have the impression that certain opposition parties are giving too much importance to this law. This law does not bring anything spectacular, nothing new to Serbia, especially nothing new to the AP Vojvodina. I agree that the Constitution of the Republic of Serbia, in Article 94, says Serbia takes care of balanced and sustainable regional development, in accordance with the law, and that the adoption of this law is necessary, that it should be passed, but this law cannot be called, even with the first step towards decentralisation and regionalisation. This law exclusively speaks of economic decentralisation and is, above all, important because of one fact, making it easier for statistical regions to access pre-accession EU funds. It is not a meaningless fact, it is very important for the economic development of the Republic of Serbia, but it cannot be said that this law on regional development is the first step towards Serbia as a decentralised and regionalised state. Before we began the debate on this law, a decision had to be made, or the Government of the Republic of Serbia should have reconsidered the decision on administrative environments. Unfortunately, these administrative districts will also have significance in this law. The borders of the administrative districts were drawn 15 years ago, in a way contrary to sound logic. Mr Dinkic, I'm sure you know, because you're coming to Saint, and to Ad, and to Kanjiž, and it's very good that you're coming, that these municipalities belong to the North-Banat Management District, and that Kanjiza and Senta are located on the Butchvan side of Tisa, so they should not belong to the North-Banat Management District. The decision was made 15 years ago because, at that time, at a republican level, the law on the selection of MPs envisioned by the majority election system was in force. The following should have been achieved, to break down that electoral body belonging to national minorities, and that is why it was done in Vojvodina, in the north of Vojvodina, in Potis and Sandzak, in no other territory of the Republic of Serbia. Unfortunately, these administrative districts, which are illogical, which are contrary to any healthy logic, have been maintained to this day. No law should be amended to change the borders of these administrative districts, it is changed by the government of the Republic of Serbia, but there seems to be no political will to change those borders. It is not a Hungarian question, and in Sandzak, it is not a Bosniak issue, it is a question of sound logic – whether Kanjiza is located in Bačka or in Banat. If Kanjiza is in Backo and Tisa hasn't changed its course, then the legal system should be in line with sound logic, because otherwise, what do we need the state for, what do we need the administrative environments for? The government hasn't considered that since 2000, it needs to be changed. Unfortunately, you, Mr Dinkic, directly transpose this system into this law on regional development. Thank God you didn't write that in the very text of the law, but you wrote it in the light of this law. On page 5 of the reasoning, it reads: "One or more areas are projected to form a region. The authorities will respect the existing division of the administrative districts and will not share the districts." This means that regions in the area will be formulated in a way contrary to sound logic and any economic and economic logic. On page 4, the reasoning reads: "The government will, on a proposal from the Republic Statistics Institute and in a scale with criteria regulated by EU standards, establish areas that make up these seven regions. We note that the Constitution, as well as the existing division of administrative districts, will be respected in the nearer determination of areas and regions, and the areas will be composed of one or more districts and regions from the area'. As for the EU standards mentioned, I do not believe that EU standards are to change river flows. I do not believe that EU standards are to define something directly contrary to sound economic logic. On the other hand, I suppose it is understood that the Constitution will be respected, it should not be written in the reasoning of the bill, but it is not good that it is written and that the Government obviously thinks that this division of administrative districts should be transferred to this second level, that this should be the basis for even regional development. This is not a good initial basis and I hope that the Ministry and Government will review these views that are written in the light of the law. We couldn't give the amendment to this, because this wasn't written in the bill itself, but in the explanation. I hope that this will be changed, in the same way that it has been amended in the adoption of the Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Law on Chambers of Commerce. We are pleased to have reached agreement with the G17 Plus Parliamentary Group and with the Government of the Republic of Serbia that sound logic, economic logic is taken into account when it comes to regional chambers of commerce. Just a few weeks ago, when we passed that law, we were able to work together to have companies from the Kanjiž, Senta and Ada municipalities re-assembled in the Regional Chamber of Commerce in Subotica, and it was very well received. I think that's the path we need to continue, not the path that's drawn in the light of the bill's proposal. As for some other proposals to be made before the law on regional development was passed, this certainly includes the Statute of the AP Vojvodina. I know this law establishes statistical regions, not administrative regions, but, Mr. Deputy Prime Minister, the problem is that the Constitution of the Republic of Serbia clearly states that a new statute should be adopted by the end of 2008. I would have one question for you, Mr Dinkic, as Vice-President of the Government of the Republic of Serbia – why do we still not have in the assembly procedure a bill establishing the jurisdiction of the AP Vojvodina? Of course we are aware that neither the Statute nor the law can arrive on the agenda before the Government sets out a draft law on the powers of the Vojvodina, but we do not understand why it is awaiting this law on the jurisdiction of the Vojvodina, which is the problem, why the Constitution of the Republic of Serbia is being violated by the Government of the Republic of Serbia and why the National Assembly is being brought into a situation that it has to violate the Constitution of the Republic of Serbia for seven months now. When we talk about the aspect of economic decentralisation that needs to be passed by this law on regional development, then I must say that we believe that the Ministry and the Government have too many powers and that neither economic regionalisation nor decentralisation can be talked about when it is decided by the Government and the Ministry. I would ask Mr. Vice-President of the Government – how can we interpret Article 17 of this bill as proposing economic decentralisation of the Republic of Serbia, otherwise, this member refers to regional development funding programmes, when it says here: "Programmes from the position of the 1st member, and projects to be financed from the Republic of Serbia's budget, as well as other sources for the financial year, brings the government, on a proposal from the Ministry, in line with the National Plan and regional strategies"? What economic decentralisation is this when the Government of the Republic of Serbia, on a proposal from the Ministry, decides on what programmes will be financed from the Republic budget? Of course, we are pleased that in the same article, in St. 4 and 5, the city of Belgrade and the AP Vojvodina will ask for an opinion on the programme on the territory of Vojvodina, or the city of Belgrade, but it is only an optional opinion. We don't think that's in line with the principle of economic decentralisation. Another law was to be passed before we discuss the law on regional development, which is the law on public property. It was certainly necessary to change the situation that says that Serbia is the only country in Europe in which neither local self-government nor autonomous provinces have their own property. Unfortunately, neither municipalities, cities, or provinces can affect the economic development of their territories and the employment of their citizens, in such a way that they can have a higher degree of financial autonomy, have their own assets, that certain land can offer, e.g. certain investors. How is it possible that the Municipality of Sainta only gets 2.5% of the collected taxes back as a transfer, and the total gets back about 7% of the amount collected in the form of taxes on the territory of the municipality of the Senate? It is one example, but it is characteristic of the entire Republic of Serbia, all municipalities and all cities. Finally, I would like to look back, very briefly, on today's statement by the president of the Republic of Boris Tadic. He says regionalisation will take place, the first stage by 2013, and the second stage by 2020. I don't know what we're going to do by 2013 or 2020. Secondly, the country's president says, "Regionisation is not a division of Serbia, but a division of wealth and the creation of opportunities for undeveloped parts of the country to develop evenly." So that's Robin Hood's principle. We should take from the rich and give to the poor. We agree, but should not be taken from the rich, a climate should be created in which all parts of the Republic of Serbia can develop. The Constitution should also be respected in connection with the Vojvodina, not to fight with every rebalancing to get Vojvodina 7%. Of course, the south of Serbia, central Serbia, eastern and western Serbia, Sandzak, the forest should develop and we should achieve as balanced regional development as possible. Thank you.

ParlaMint-RS_2009-06-09-0.u21712	To answer these two questions Mr. Balint Pastor and this last one. First, to answer the questions that Balint Pastor asked. He asked about the definition of administrative districts. As you know, it's not the Ministry of Economy's jurisdiction, it's the Ministry of State Administration and local self-government. When we were preparing this law, when we defined the areas, we couldn't help but start from the existing administrative districts. However, as Mr. Pastor said, it is possible to change it, even by a legal act. I propose to Mr. Pastor that in the coming days, when Minister Markovic interprets the law on associations, he discusses this issue concerning Kanjiza. As we have found a solution to the chambers of commerce, I believe it is possible to find this solution as well. Indeed, it is not up to this law, but if the Regulation on the definition of administrative districts is amended, it is automatically understood – and statistical areas, in accordance with this law. As for the issue relating to Article 17, I think it is about misunderstanding, because the question is asked – why is the government making a decision on funding from the RS budget in the region's funding programme? Because it's about budgets, about the Republic of Serbia's budget. The government, under the Law on the Budget System, must make a decision, but the proposal is made by those sitting in the regional council. The regional strategy is the basis for a project to be proposed, and the government only adopts it, given that it is financing it. In the Vojvodina case, an AP opinion is required, because this is about funding from republican funds. Of course, when the province funds from its own resources, it does not ask the Republic for an opinion, but invests itself in its own project. The same goes for the city of Belgrade. Of course there's an asymmetry here. Asymmetry stems, unfortunately, from the fact that there is currently only one active administrative region in Serbia, which is Vojvodina. When that changes one day and the concept of administrative regionalisation is established, then this will be adjusted in law itself. This way, we started with the existing organisation of territorialization of Serbia and did not want to interfere with the jurisdiction defined by another law, or the Constitution. As for Mr Balint Pastor's questions regarding the deadline for the adoption of the Statute of Vojvodina and the law on the transfer of jurisdiction, according to my findings, and I am not directly involved in it, however, is my colleague Kalanovic, the job is technically completed. I now expect that this will be adopted in the government in the coming period and proposed to the Assembly. As far as I know, there is no point that is not in accordance with the Law on the Transfer of Jurisdiction and the Statute of the Region. In that sense, I expect it to be adopted in the next period. The profit may not be too large, it is 722 euros per full, however, 722 euros is better than loss. Capacity is now increasing, along with investments that we and the Italians took in Lakirnica, and the capacity will soon be increased to 3,000 cars per month. With a capacity of 3,000 cars a month, Kragujevac will, after 20 or 15 years, be removed from the list of subsidies from the economy ministry. In this month, June, we reduced by 25 million dinars of subsidies that have been paid to the Kragujevac factory for years, and our goal is to ensure that complete production in Kragujevac is achieved on the basis of the car sales revenues charged. There are these str's that go into blockade, start new firms and keep working, so the number itself is big, essentially, the problem exists, but it's not what you're saying. We did what was possible to improve the liquidity of the economy, through the government's programme of measures, subsidized loans. About 400m euros were granted to businesses at low interest in three months. Yesterday, the Central Bank shot down the reference interest to 13%, so now the dinar loans, after a long time, will be single-digit for those seeking liquidity loans, will be 9.5%, since they are related to the central bank's reference rate. The problem of regionalisation in Serbia is, by the way, subtlely, complex and very important constitutionally and, I would say, a couple of excellent political issues, in order to settle any law that is not a fundamental law in this area by the way. The possible problem of this kind can be met already in Article 1 of the proposed law, which suggests that the law will regulate – the names of the region, the way in which regions make up, how units of local self-government are determined, etc. It would not be good if the law on regional development of the office of matter which, in principle, does not belong to this area, and I think that certain formulations should be carefully reviewed before the law is definitely passed. This law, in my opinion, can only be directed to one aspect of regionalism in our country, which is economic decentralisation, i.e. regional development policy, and this should be maintained, because regionalisation is a much more complex problem than the economic aspect of regionalisation, which is one component. Because of the need to clarify the many misunderstandings and controversys that may arise in connection with this law, I have the impression that no other political issue at this time is holding so much attention to domestic political issues, I would say, and to the general public, such as the issue of decentralisation and regionalisation in Serbia. It would be said that the global economic crisis, which, like all other monsters, does not select victims, has helped significantly. The largest, in my opinion, among the local victims, were local self-governments, cities, municipalities of Southeastern Serbia, which have been exposed to economic collapse for a long time and whose majority of citizens are on the verge of total poverty. Some kind of rescue light at the end of the tunnel, so to speak, the domestic crisis could be, right now, decentralisation. But in modern democratic states, there is also a strong process of regionalisation, which implies the transfer of certain central authorities to certain regional or lower authorities, but which will continue to have a state character. There are, as is known, many definitions of the region and I think it should be said very precisely that regions are often of their own kind, and geographical and historical, clearly recognizable internal formations, which are characterized by certain ethnic, cultural and linguistic and dialectic specificities, but also by a whole series of other features of political, economic, demographic, socio-psychological order, which cannot be precisely recognized in this law, probably because it speaks of only one function of the region, which is a development component. In the case of Serbia, regionalisation would be adapted to the specificities of our legal-political system. Since such regionalisation of the state must rest necessarily on the accompanying principle of demetropolisation, the form would also be applied to regional centres towards local communities in the region, thus preventing overdominating one environment from being dominated by others, which is a very common case in Serbia. In order for regionalisation to be successful, it must necessarily be followed by decentralisation in the field of fiscal policy, which means that the money collected from taxpayers would be significantly more justly deployed than is now the case, so all regional environments would have the same chance for accelerated development in all areas of social life, which I hope is the intensity of this law. The regions, in any sense, should by no means have the attributes of autonomous statehood, because they would be clearly and unequivocally subordinated to the Constitution and to the law envisaged in the state. This means that there must be no talk of regionalisation as a prelude to the fragmentation of the state, nor would the creation of a region motivate the operation of a hidden separatist force, ready to threaten Serbia's territorial integrity. Regionalisation should, in my opinion, be a necessary condition for democratic progress and for the economic improvement of our entire country, to enable citizens to live better and to exercise their rights more effectively. The accent should be functional, not administrative regionalization. This is a European approach and there are good European experiences, which we should apply to us. However, I am one of those people who think that at this point is much more important than the process of regionalisation of the decentralisation process, demetropolisation and strengthening of local self-government, which are the preconditions for achieving both the economic and political policentrism processes in the Republic. In doing so, it seems to me that two processes must be achieved in sync: balanced economic development and strengthening the position of citizens in the country. I think that when we discuss Serbia's regionalisation, in any way, we need to be very careful; we should avoid a trap to impose a country's atomisation process through regionalisation, which could be a latent and real danger. Regionalisation can also potentially lead to further institutionalisation of state functions, only at a lower level, and that must be taken into account. Regionalisation, in my opinion, must not endanger the established classical functions of the state and should not lead to the establishment of so-called. the mini-states at regional level should already lead to the state's effective functioning and citizens exercising their rights in quality. I think we should first pass this law on regional development, which we are now doing, but, complementarily with that, I have talked about this in the context of public debate, and there must be a law on the transfer of state affairs to municipalities and cities, and in a greater amount, more than ever, because if we want to achieve a policy of equal regional development, then we need to give jurisdiction to lower units of local self-government, that they can have mechanisms to achieve this policy of equal regional development. Finally, in Serbia's 50-year-old recent story, there were different forms of regionalism, including the period when there were no intermedial forms of organisation between the Republic and municipalities. There is experience, I think, that they must be carefully analysed, because the issue of a country's territorial organisation is a question of whom they don't approach so often. Now I'd like to look at this component of the so-called. statistical regions, which are in the proposal for a law on regional development, because I think we need to regulate the approach to functional regionalisation through these so-called so-called "regional development" statistical regions as a form of regionalisation; other forms are possible, other modes, perhaps even better than this, but I don't mind such a formulation. The statistical region can exist as a form of economic decentralisation, but in an extremely limited extent. Since this law does not prejudge the territorial order of the state, although it may be understood, which could only be governed by the Constitution, which is clear to all, statistical regions, at least I, consider only as a form to provide EU funds for regional development, in no other way than that. This concept should be seen as an opportunity to reach funds for faster regional development, as any other observation of statistical regions as possible forms of administrative decentralisation is a completely wrong approach. In addition, in Article 4 of this proposal of the law, statistical regions are defined as – statisticalally functional territorial continents and, rightly, because of possible misunderstandings and misunderstandings, it is not about administrative territorial units. In this context, it is specifically proposed for the discussion to be a solution in Article 6 of this Law, that the Government determines the units of local self-government and urban municipalities that make up the area, so, in my opinion, that this can be arranged differently, because this can be included in the territorial organisation of the Republic, which is not good From the third chapter, then the matter of regional development should be properly arranged, with the fact that I do not understand why in Chapter V, which speaks of regional development entities, only the competences of the Autonomous Province of Vojvodina and the capital, but not the municipalities and cities in Serbia. Whether they are not considered important subjects of regional development, or something else is at stake, I don't know, but if it stays that way, it can create the impression that it is inclining again towards economic metropolisation, not economic politicintriism, which is the basic idea of this law. Finally, with certain adjustments to be made, I consider this law to be an initial step towards Serbia's full regional development, but it cannot and must not be prepared for Serbia's future administrative regionalisation, and it must be clearly said, because then and otherwise it could be talked about the proposed law if the motives were second. In the end, the idea of this law should be seen not as a crossover of Serbia's territorial organisation, but as a strengthening of economic policentrism and economic decentralisation in Serbia, because by applying it, one process, which will be long-term and long-term, would begin to reduce differences not in poverty, but in the economic status of certain parts of Serbia, which is what it repeatedly points to from undeveloped areas from the south and southeast of Serbia, from areas where the state has not invested enough in their development. The adoption of this law is, in fact, the process of changing economic policy, in terms of even regional development, and this is a good way to support it.

Certainly, Serbia does not fit the administrative regionalisation system Germany has. Germany is a large state, a federal state, and it is certain that a country with eight million inhabitants, like Serbia, cannot go that way. If you look at other countries, each one in its own way has been regulated by the area of administrative regionalisation, which also means the transfer of rights and responsibilities to regions. Regions don't rule out the municipality, but they are, right, one interstep to the central level of government. In some countries, which are larger, there are as many as four intersteps, and that is probably not good. Italy, for example, has a central state and local self-government and specific autonomous areas and regions in Italy, and it is indeed a system that, as far as I'm concerned, is complicated. But in most countries there are at least three levels, one is the level of the municipality, the second is the level of the region and, of course, the central government. Administrative regionalisation will also mean that the collection of certain tax revenues is left to regions, such as now one part of the tax revenues of the AP Vojvodina, regardless of whether we have the provinces, or we will have one province and the other areas. Whatever it is called, the essence of regionalisation is that these people living in the locals are allowed to raise funds and be responsible for developing their own region, and that is then a motive for these citizens to choose their local and regional authorities to develop their own region more quickly. Another jurisdiction is, as a rule, transferred to regions in administrative decentralisation, which is the competence in the adoption of certain rules. Ecology regulations, from areas related to local culture, are passed in regions, not central congregations. Central congregations make decisions regarding peace, security, the central budget, for areas of importance for the whole country, while regions determine some important issues for them, and relatively irrelevant for the central level, because environmental issues are not the same, for example, in southern Serbia and in the north of Vojvodina. There are different problems, and each one settles his problems with his own regulations. Of course Serbia will someday come to that. Why? Not because I want to, but because it's inevitable. ParlaMint-RS_2009-06-09-0.u21748	Mrs. Susana Grubjesic has a word. Here you go.
ParlaMint-RS_2009-06-09-0.u21749	Thank you, Madam President. Dear People's MPs, Your Honor, I'll deal with the bill, I don't want to continue this debate, I think it's quite said on this subject. With the 2006 constitution, Serbia is conceived as a centralised state, both politically and financially, with the province's scarce competencies and the very small competencies of local self-government. Modern states, in turn, are decentralisationd because experience and theory have shown that a complex state cannot be managed successfully and managed from one center. Vojvodina has received certain powers compared to the rest of the country, so that Serbia today is a centralised state, in which Vojvodina is the only defined region, or autonomous province, while the second province, Kosovo and Metohija, as we know, has been under international administration since 1999 and based on UN Resolution 1244. This system certainly cannot survive in the longer run, as decentralisation, regionalisation and even regional development are a prerequisite for future development of each country, including Serbia. The first concrete step in regionalisation, but unfortunately insufficient, is to adopt exactly this law on regional development, as the objectives of creating such regions should be balanced regional development, a greater degree of competitiveness and functional integration in the environment. This kind of regionalisation does not have political motives, because regions are statistical, not administrative. I've got to kick some ass. Statistical regions are not a condition for Serbia to move closer to the EU, but countries that want to join the EU are expected to align internal division to regionalisation regions within two years of signing the SAA. The European Union does not force any country to do so, nor does it give criteria and suggestions on how to do so, but offers carrots for such a reform, and carrots are European funds. One of the commitments Serbia has made by signing the SAA is to deliver data to the European Commission on the level of gross social product per capita, harmonised at NUTS 2 level of the region, over a period of four years. The existence of a NUTS division in the accession phase allows for more efficient policy-making of regional development, since it provides clear indicators of the state's state of development and thus creates a space for selecting the most vulnerable measures to address regional proportionality and underdevelopment. We need the regions not only to meet the standards and requirements for the allocation of EU funds, but to have the necessary preconditions for the overall development of our economy within one territory. Statistical regions cannot possibly be political regions, as the law does not foresee the formation of regional authorities. You can't see anywhere that the regions will get government, assembly, administrative authorities. Such regionalisation does not involve any autonomy, but the marking of cities and municipalities entering the remit of regional agencies, which will co-ordinate their projects with Belgrade and with EU funds. Euroregion will not be administrative territory, will not have legal subjectivity, but each will have its own regional development agency, which will be in charge of implementing regional development policy. Above regional levels, three more levels of national agencies are foreseen to carry out and control the same area. The government of Serbia, its institutions, will have full control over national agencies, i.e. regional agencies, so that the statistical Euroregion will not have autonomy, even of the funds approved by European funds for regional projects. The regions should themselves develop regional programmes and projects financed by money from European funds, which means developing appropriate administrative capacity, programming capacity and financial management. Statistical regions, already said several times, are formed according to the nomenclature of territorial statistical units of NUTS, which is the new EU statistical model, as the statistical services of the EU candidate countries agreed with Eurostat as early as 1997, which is the Statistical Institute of the European Community in Luxembourg, to establish NUTS nomenclature 5, according to the number of inhabitants, i.e. the size of the territorial community. This was later established by the European Commission Regulation since May 2003. The NUTS country's adaptability makes it easier to join the EU, enables access to structural funding funds for the programmes, which has proved essential for the countries of Eastern and Central Europe. According to the draft law we are talking about today, there are relevant levels 2, that is the region, and 3, that is the area of the nomenclature of statistical territorial units prepared by the Republic Statistics Institute and adopted by the Government. Let me also say something about the EU's regional policy, as little has been said about it today. The main purpose of regional policy is to help less developed regions, help with their development, which should bring the EU's overall development into line. Regional policy also includes cohesion policy, as well as parts of social and agricultural policy and, in a narrow sense, concerns reducing regional differences. As a common EU policy, regional policy was launched in 1975. At the time it was launched, the biggest income difference between developed and undeveloped regions was exactly 7:1, as is the case with Serbia. The most developed region was the province of Hamburg, and the most underdeveloped was Calabria. Today, the lowest income of 271 NUTS 2 regions, as many as there are in the EU, has the lowest income in the Southeast Romania region, with the highest income in London. Regional policy is based on several principles and these principles can be applied without any changes and additions to Serbia. It is, above all, an additionality, which means that, in addition to the Union's funds, national or local governments, they should also secure their share of the funds. The second principle is proportionality, which means more funding is given for projects that are considered more significant. The third principle is a partnership, which means that projects are implemented under the leadership of bodies, which, in addition to the representatives of the Union, are attended by representatives of the interested party, whether by state, region or municipality. Regional policies are implemented by means of funds concentrated in two funds, which are cohesion and structural. In addition to agriculture, these are the largest funds in the Union, which account for 36% of the total Union funds, but for countries such as Serbia, which have not yet won membership candidateship, the Union set up an IPA fund in 2007 – an instrument for pre-accession assistance, replacing, previously, several funds that have been abolished, such as PAR, SEPARD, ISPA, KARC etc. IPA assistance is given for the following purposes: firstly, for building institutions and rule of law, secondly, regional co-operation, thirdly, cross-border co-operation, fourthly, human resources and, fifthly, for rural development. The candidate countries, which are Croatia, Macedonia and Turkey, can apply for funding within all five items, while other Western Balkan countries, Serbia, BiH, Albania and Montenegro can only compete for the first two items -- building institutions and the rule of law and regional co-operation. Statistical regions should gradually learn to manage themselves, so that they can serve as the basis for future political regions. Everything is said in the Condicional – it could serve and could also lead to political regionalisation. This regionalisation of Serbia is still an open issue, although it is increasingly present among lawyers, especially constitutionalists, politicians, sociologists, and most of all among politicians. In the professional public, this idea has both advocates and opponents. That regionalisation leads to federalization, and federalization towards a possible collapse of the state, is one of the basic thesis of opponents of regionalization. Of course, demagogue politicians use much harder words, so every attempt to regionalize is called breaking the state, betraying something and someone, but I won't talk about it much, we've heard a lot about it from my predecessors. Economists express another type of reserve, ask the question – how much will regionalisation cost? The complex system of the organisation of the authorities entails higher costs of administration and funding of work and services at all levels, as well as the danger of jeopardising the single market. I will add that there is also a danger of feudalisation, of provincialization, of strengthening localism of all kinds and of multiplying administration and bureaucracy, but all this is being resolved and prevented by good legal solutions, and even more by implementing these same good laws. There are internal and foreign policy reasons for regionalisation, but problems in Serbia's regionalisation can and must be approached only by looking at the overall territory, inherited experience, the experience of countries that have been past, or by similar problems, and especially in view of the process of integration on the European continent. The existing social and territorial arrangements do not allow the fundamental principle of equality of all citizens of Serbia to be achieved for regional organisation. So far, solutions have given this right only to some citizens, and it is a vertical division of each other's powers, compared to the other. Serbia has since its first constitution, i.e. the first postwar constitution, in 1946, to this day, been an asymmetric state, with two autonomous provinces, Vojvodina and Kosovo and Metohija, of which the second, as we know, is under the protection of the international community. The territory between is undefined, is often referred to as the rest of Serbia, or remains nameless. Regardless of the fact that this so-called. The rest of Serbia has no such express historical, traditional, cultural, ethnic characteristics as Vojvodina and Kosovo and Metohija, it must in no way challenge the legitimate right of citizens living in that territory, which connects geography, economy, energy, traffic, natural resources and the like, to regional organisation. Asymmetry is dissatisfied with citizens living in the provinces and those living in the so-called so-called area. the rest of Serbia, because such imbalance is unsustainable in the longer term. This asymmetric status, which was pre-guaranteed only for parts of the territory, was, and now, not to lie, the potential factor of disintegration of state territory. Such unbalanced economic and social development and huge differences between rich and poor areas, 7-1, the differences between the richest and poorest municipalities of 15:1 cannot be reduced without regionalisation and without the transfer of competence to lower levels of government. Local and regional democracy is the basis of a democratic society, as Serbia wants to become at the most recent time. For foreign policy reasons, I'll mention the following. The process of European integration for which Serbia has been defined is inseparable from regionalism. The European Union is now resting on the philosophy of Euro-regionalism as a form of cross-border integration of neighbouring areas, districts or regions. Many countries have been on their way to the EU through the process of structural reforms and adjustments, achieving different regionalisation models. Brussels supports the creation of the region, because they are a prerequisite for using EU funds, both for Member States, those are the structural funds I mentioned, and for candidate countries, they are pre-accession funds. The European Regional Assembly was established back in 1985 and numbers 250 regions from 26 countries in Europe and 12 interregional organisations. The Council of Europe, which Serbia joined in 2003, cited as one of the main tasks as decentralisation of state authorities, improving local and regional autonomy. One of the most important bodies of the Council of Europe, in addition to the Parliamentary Assembly, the Council of Ministers, the European Court of Human Rights, is the Congress of Local and Regional Authorities of Europe. The Council of Europe seeks to influence the regionalisation of European states through this congress, especially the newly admitted countries of Eastern Europe and the Western Balkans. The institutional integration of the region through European institutions opens up the possibility for citizens of these regions to influence European policy-making through their representatives, but such a possibility is given only to Vojvodina, as a member of the Assembly of the European Regions, while so-called The rest of Serbia must priorly be territorially and administratively reorganised in order to gain such rights. So, the regionalisation goal is to ensure greater control of the authorities' representatives and ease of exchange, and, in the end, regionalisation is also a democratic response to the separatist aspirations that could eventually arise in some parts of Serbia. So, to conclude, this statistical regionalisation, in fact, involves no regions in the right sense of that word, but the marking of municipalities and cities that are entering the competences of regional agencies, which will co-ordinate with Belgrade and EU accession funds. As long as the region is not administrative, until it has its own account and its own administration, it is only a geographical area, under the direction of a centralist-oriented state. As far as the G17 Plus party is concerned, we are starting with the fact that Serbian citizens can meet their real needs and interests through the authorities and institutions closest to them, which have the greatest democratic potential, or whose control and exchangeability is easier. All Serbian citizens must have the same right to regional organisation. The vertical division of government in Serbia cannot remain a privilege for one another. To repeat, this means that historic, cultural and ethnic traits that have led to the territorial autonomy of Vojvodina do not mean giving up the right to regionalise other parts of Serbia. The creation of the region should have pragmatic goals, which would benefit society and the state as a whole, but we are all aware of the fact that Serbia's decision on regionalisation cannot and must not be made to a shortcut, but with which it extends political and social consensus, because Serbia must not be a state in which people are divided into the citizens of the first and second order, because all of us living in Serbia have the right to dignity and equal conditions. For such a Serbia, G17 plus advocates and therefore we support the adoption of a law on regional development, as the first, though I repeat, insufficient steps to regionalise Serbia. Thank you.

The first thing is that it allows the original sovereignty of citizens, which they transfer to local self-government, to regional authorities and to the state in a better way and to achieve their essential interests. At the same time, the issue of regionalisation is also important because of the more even development of the country. We deeply believe that a country that contains huge differences in the degree of development is a country that is unstable, is a country where disintegration processes are strengthened, not weak.
